[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you for seeing me. I... I guess I'm here because my eating has just gotten way out of hand lately. You see, I work down at the production plant, and they've had me on these late shifts for a while now. It really messes with your body clock, you know? You get off work, it's pitch black out, you're completely exhausted, your feet are killing you... and well, I'll stop for takeout on the way home. \n\nI always tell myself it'll just be a quick bite to take the edge off, but then I get to the drive-thru and I end up ordering, like, two whole burgers, a huge order of fries, a thick shake, and I'll just put it all away in the car. And you'd think I'd be completely stuffed after all that, right? But then I get home, and I don't even go straight inside the house to go to bed. I go into the garage—we have this second refrigerator out there where we keep the extra groceries and Tupperware stuff—and I'll just stand there and clean out whatever leftovers I can find. It's literally like I'm eating a whole second supper right there in the garage. I just... I don't know why I do it, but it's becoming a real problem and I just can't seem to stop myself once I get going.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of overeating, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to 'undo' the calories you consumed?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I know some people do, you see it on TV or read about it in magazines, where folks will make themselves sick or take a bunch of pills to clear themselves out. But I've never done that. I couldn't stomach the thought of making myself throw up, to be honest. It's just not something I could bring myself to do. \n\nAnd as for exercise... well, by the time I've finished my shift at the plant, and then had my little drive-thru feast and my garage raid, I'm just too wiped out. My knees aren't what they used to be, you know? Being on concrete floors for eight, sometimes ten hours a day, it really takes a toll on the joints. So the idea of going for a run or hitting a gym after all that eating... it's just out of the question. I usually just end up dragging myself into the house, feeling so full and heavy, and just collapsing into my recliner. I'll sit there, feeling miserable about what I just did, my stomach stretched tight like a drum, but I just sit there and let it settle. I don't try to get rid of it or work it off, I just... sit with it. And feel terrible about it.\n\nDoctor: During these times when you're eating a lot, do you feel like you're losing control — like you can't stop eating even if you want to?\n\nPatient: That is exactly it. It's... well, it's honestly the most frustrating part of the whole ordeal, because I know what I'm doing while I'm doing it, if that makes any sense. Like, I'm fully aware that I've already eaten enough to feed a family of four in the front seat of my truck, but once I start, it feels like a switch is just flipped in my brain. \n\nI'll be standing out there in the chilly garage, staring into the fridge, and my mind is practically screaming at me to stop. I'm literally standing there, telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just close the refrigerator door, turn off the light, and go to bed.' I mean, I'm a grown man, you know? I'm fifty-four years old, I manage a whole section of guys down at the plant, I make decisions all night long, so I should be able to just walk away from a piece of Tupperware, right? \n\nBut it's like... it's like my hands don't even belong to me anymore. They just keep reaching for more. I'll grab another piece of cold chicken, or scoop out some leftover casserole, and I'm chewing and swallowing while that voice in my head is just begging me to quit. But I can't. The brakes are completely gone. I just keep going and going until there's nothing left in the container, even though I'm so full it physically hurts and I'm exhausted from my shift. It's a terrible feeling, just watching yourself do something you desperately want to stop doing, but you just... you just can't seem to make your hands stop moving.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control while eating happen? Are they something that happens multiple times a week, or more spread out?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about how often, because I was actually just trying to figure that out myself the other day. I was looking at my bank statements—because, let me tell you, all those drive-thru runs really start to add up, and my wife, she handles most of the finances, so I have to be careful about what I'm putting on the card—and I realized it's been happening pretty consistently for the past few months now. \n\nI'd say, at the very least, there's always one night a week where I just completely lose it. Usually, it's a Friday or whatever my Friday happens to be on that week's rotation, when the exhaustion just really catches up to me. But honestly, more often than not lately, it's been creeping up to two, sometimes even three times a week. It really depends on how the shifts are going. Like, if we're doing a big push to meet a quota and the line is just moving non-stop, and my supervisor is breathing down my neck... those are the nights I'm almost guaranteed to pull into that drive-thru and then hit the garage fridge. \n\nIt's like, the rougher the day, the more likely I am to end up standing out there in the cold, eating cold casserole out of a plastic tub. And it's just been a steady pattern for months now. I keep hoping it'll slow down when the schedule lightens up, but then we get a new order in, or someone calls out sick and I have to cover, and before I know it, I'm right back in that same cycle, two or three nights a week, just eating until I can't breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating make you feel very upset, interfere with your daily life, or affect things like your relationships or how you function at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot of things, really. Upsetting? Yeah, I'd say that's an understatement. Like I said, I sit there in my recliner and just feel terrible. It eats at me, no pun intended. But as for my daily life, it's complicated. I mean, I haven't missed a shift at the plant. I'm still clocking in, doing my job, making sure the line keeps moving. I take my responsibilities seriously, you know? And I still go to church on Sundays with my wife, shake hands, smile, do all the things you're supposed to do. I haven't let it completely derail my life in that sense. \n\nBut at home... that's where things are getting, well, strained. I've been pulling away, being a lot more withdrawn. And it's exhausting, honestly. It takes so much energy just to cover my tracks. I'll get home after my drive-thru run and my garage binge, and my wife will have dinner waiting, or she'll ask if I want to eat, and I have to come up with these excuses. I'll tell her I had a big lunch, or that my stomach is feeling a little off from something I ate at the cafeteria, or that I'm just too tired to eat right now. And it's lying. I'm lying to my wife, who I love dearly, just to hide what I'm doing. \n\nAnd then I have to make sure the evidence is gone. I'm burying fast-food wrappers at the bottom of the trash can outside, rearranging the Tupperware in the fridge so it doesn't look like half the casserole is missing... it's like a full-time job just keeping the secret. So, yeah, I'm functioning, I'm working, I'm showing up to church, but at home, I'm just... I'm not really there. I'm too busy managing this whole hidden life, and it's definitely putting a wall up between me and my wife. It's just a heavy, heavy thing to carry around all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes ever caused you to avoid social situations, feel embarrassed about your eating habits, or affected your performance or concentration at work?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as work goes, no, I wouldn't say it's affecting my performance or my concentration on the floor. Like I said, I take my job down at the plant very seriously. When I'm on the clock, I'm focused on the line, making sure my guys are doing what they need to do, checking the machinery... I don't really let my mind wander to food when I'm in the middle of a shift. The problem is always after the shift, you know? \n\nBut as for feeling embarrassed and avoiding social things... yeah. Yeah, absolutely. That's a huge part of it. And it's not even just big social events, it's right in my own house. I mean, my wife is a wonderful cook, she really is, and she always makes sure there's a good, hot meal ready. But I'll sit there at the table with her, and I'll just kind of pick at my food, or I'll take a really small portion. I'll put on this whole act, saying things like, \"Oh, I'm just not that hungry tonight,\" or \"I had a really heavy lunch at the cafeteria,\" when the truth is, I'm starving. But I can't eat the way I want to eat in front of her. I just can't. \n\nI know if I started eating the amount I actually want to eat, she'd look at me like I'd lost my mind. I'd be so incredibly embarrassed for her to see how much I'm actually putting away. So, I play it cool at the dinner table, act like a bird picking at seeds, and then... then I wait. I'll wait until she's busy watching her shows in the living room, or until she goes upstairs to take a bath, or sometimes I'll even just make an excuse to go out to the truck to 'check on something.' And that's when I do it. That's when I eat alone, where nobody can see me, because the thought of anyone, especially my wife, watching me shovel all that food into my mouth... it's just humiliating. It really is. I just can't bear the thought of anyone seeing me like that, completely out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes happen even when you’re not particularly stressed or tired, or do they usually happen only after a really rough day at work or other stressful situations?\n\nPatient: You know, that's a really good question, and honestly... I don't rightly know. I mean, I sit here and try to turn that over in my head, and it's hard to say because, well, what even is a day without stress or being tired lately? Working down at the plant, even on a supposedly \"good\" day, you're still standing on your feet for eight, sometimes ten hours straight on that hard concrete floor. Your lower back aches, your knees are barking by the time you finally clock out. So there's always some baseline level of exhaustion, you know? It's just part of the job, part of getting older, I guess. \n\nBut... do I do it when I'm actually feeling completely fine? When I'm rested and relaxed? I'm trying to think back to, like, a regular Saturday. Saturdays, if I don't pick up an overtime shift, we usually just take it easy. Me and the wife might putter around the house, I might mow the lawn or finally get around to fixing that squeaky hinge on the back screen door... just normal, quiet, everyday stuff. Am I stressed then? Not really. Not at all, actually. But do I still end up sneaking out to the garage fridge later that night? I... I'm just not sure. I haven't really paid close enough attention to whether it only happens after the rough shifts. \n\nLike I said before, those bad days with the quotas and the supervisor breathing down my neck, those are definitely the times I know for a fact I'm going to lose it. But as for the good days, or the easy days... I just haven't noticed if there's a strict rule to it. It all kind of blurs together after a while, to be perfectly honest with you. You get into this routine, this cycle of hiding and eating, and it just becomes a habit, almost like muscle memory. So, I guess I really don't have a solid answer for you on that one. I'd have to really stop and think about it and maybe track if it happens on a quiet weekend.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much faster than usual, or continuing to eat even when you're uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Faster than usual doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... it's almost like a panic, you know? When I pull into the driveway after a shift, if I've stopped at the drive-thru, I'll usually just stay parked right there in the cab of my truck. Or, if I'm already home, I'll take whatever I scrounged up out to my workshop in the backyard, tell my wife I need to organize my wrenches or check on a project. And once I'm out there... man, I just tear into it. \n\nI eat so fast in my truck or out in the workshop that half the time I barely taste it. It's just... shove it in, chew once or twice, swallow, grab the next thing. It's exactly like I’m trying to get it down before somebody catches me. Because that's the real fear, right? I'm sitting out there, constantly looking over my shoulder, watching the back windows of the house to see if the kitchen blinds move or if the porch light clicks on. If I hear the neighbor's dog bark or the wind rattle the aluminum siding on the garage, my heart just jumps right up into my throat. \n\nSo I'm just rushing, rushing, rushing. I'll have a sandwich in one hand and a handful of whatever else in the other, and I'm not even enjoying the meal. You'd think if a guy is going to go through all this trouble, all this lying and sneaking around to get the food, he'd at least sit back and savor it, wouldn't you? But no. It's just this frantic blur. I'll end up getting grease on my steering wheel or dropping crumbs all over my workbench because I'm just not paying attention to anything except making the food disappear as fast as humanly possible. I'm just terrified she's going to walk out that back screen door and catch me stuffing my face like a madman. And because I'm eating at that kind of speed, just shoveling it in like coal into a furnace, I don't even realize how much I've put away until it's all gone and I'm sitting there feeling like I'm going to burst.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these episodes because you’re embarrassed about how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was touching on earlier, that is exactly why I do it alone. It's the embarrassment, plain and simple. I mean, picture this: we're sitting at the table, right? She's made a beautiful pot roast, maybe some of those glazed carrots she does, the house smells fantastic, and I'm sitting there acting like I'm just not that hungry. I'll literally put on a performance. I'll say, \"Oh honey, this is great, but they had a big spread down at the cafeteria today for somebody's retirement,\" or whatever lie comes to mind. I'll take a tiny little portion, practically eating like a bird, pushing it around my plate. \n\nBut on the inside? I'm starving. I just know that if I ate what I really wanted to eat right then and there, she would be horrified. I mean, she'd probably think I had a tapeworm or something, the way I can put it away. So I just can't do it in front of her. I'd be way too embarrassed for her—or anyone, really—to see how much I'm actually putting away. \n\nSo I just wait it out. I sit there and I wait until she's distracted, maybe she's watching her crime shows in the living room or she's on the phone with her sister, and then I'll make my excuse and sneak out to the truck or the workshop. I have to be alone. If I'm alone, there's no judgment, no wide eyes staring at me wondering when I'm going to stop. It's just me hidden away, because the thought of an audience for that... man, I couldn't handle the shame of it. I really couldn't. It's just too much to explain, so it's easier to just hide it out in the driveway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{
  "candidates": ["F50.8", "F50.9"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S049\n  name: Eating_Large_Amount\n  description: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that after late shifts I’ll stop for takeout and end up eating two burgers, fries, a shake, and then still go home and clean out leftovers in the garage like it’s a second supper.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I start, it feels like the switch is flipped and even while I’m telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag and go inside,' my hands just keep reaching for more.\n- key: S051\n  name: Rapid_Eating\n  description: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n  manifestation: I eat so fast in my truck or out in the workshop that half the time I barely taste it, like I’m trying to get it down before somebody catches me.\n- key: S052\n  name: Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full\n  description: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n  manifestation: By the time I stop, my stomach is tight against my work pants and I’m sitting there leaned back, uncomfortable enough that I don’t want to move for a while.\n- key: S054\n  name: Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment\n  description: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n  manifestation: I’ll act like I’m not that hungry at dinner with my wife, then later I’ll go out to the truck or wait until she’s busy because I’d be embarrassed for her to see how much I’m putting away.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: Afterward I feel ashamed and kind of disgusted with myself, because I’ve always thought of myself as disciplined and dependable, and this makes me feel like I’m sneaking around and letting myself down.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For the past few months, there’s usually at least one night a week—and often two or three—where I end up doing this after a late shift or a rough day on the line.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still getting to work and keeping up with church, but I’ve been more withdrawn at home because I’m spending energy covering up where I stopped and why I’m not hungry for dinner.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about five months, since I was put in the acting lead role on the CNC line.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do these episodes happen even when you’re not particularly stressed or tired, or do they usually happen only after a really rough day at work or other stressful situations?\n\nPatient: You know, that's a really good question, and honestly... I don't rightly know. I mean, I sit here and try to turn that over in my head, and it's hard to say because, well, what even is a day without stress or being tired lately? Working down at the plant, even on a supposedly \"good\" day, you're still standing on your feet for eight, sometimes ten hours straight on that hard concrete floor. Your lower back aches, your knees are barking by the time you finally clock out. So there's always some baseline level of exhaustion, you know? It's just part of the job, part of getting older, I guess. \n\nBut... do I do it when I'm actually feeling completely fine? When I'm rested and relaxed? I'm trying to think back to, like, a regular Saturday. Saturdays, if I don't pick up an overtime shift, we usually just take it easy. Me and the wife might putter around the house, I might mow the lawn or finally get around to fixing that squeaky hinge on the back screen door... just normal, quiet, everyday stuff. Am I stressed then? Not really. Not at all, actually. But do I still end up sneaking out to the garage fridge later that night? I... I'm just not sure. I haven't really paid close enough attention to whether it only happens after the rough shifts. \n\nLike I said before, those bad days with the quotas and the supervisor breathing down my neck, those are definitely the times I know for a fact I'm going to lose it. But as for the good days, or the easy days... I just haven't noticed if there's a strict rule to it. It all kind of blurs together after a while, to be perfectly honest with you. You get into this routine, this cycle of hiding and eating, and it just becomes a habit, almost like muscle memory. So, I guess I really don't have a solid answer for you on that one. I'd have to really stop and think about it and maybe track if it happens on a quiet weekend.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much faster than usual, or continuing to eat even when you're uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Faster than usual doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... it's almost like a panic, you know? When I pull into the driveway after a shift, if I've stopped at the drive-thru, I'll usually just stay parked right there in the cab of my truck. Or, if I'm already home, I'll take whatever I scrounged up out to my workshop in the backyard, tell my wife I need to organize my wrenches or check on a project. And once I'm out there... man, I just tear into it. \n\nI eat so fast in my truck or out in the workshop that half the time I barely taste it. It's just... shove it in, chew once or twice, swallow, grab the next thing. It's exactly like I’m trying to get it down before somebody catches me. Because that's the real fear, right? I'm sitting out there, constantly looking over my shoulder, watching the back windows of the house to see if the kitchen blinds move or if the porch light clicks on. If I hear the neighbor's dog bark or the wind rattle the aluminum siding on the garage, my heart just jumps right up into my throat. \n\nSo I'm just rushing, rushing, rushing. I'll have a sandwich in one hand and a handful of whatever else in the other, and I'm not even enjoying the meal. You'd think if a guy is going to go through all this trouble, all this lying and sneaking around to get the food, he'd at least sit back and savor it, wouldn't you? But no. It's just this frantic blur. I'll end up getting grease on my steering wheel or dropping crumbs all over my workbench because I'm just not paying attention to anything except making the food disappear as fast as humanly possible. I'm just terrified she's going to walk out that back screen door and catch me stuffing my face like a madman. And because I'm eating at that kind of speed, just shoveling it in like coal into a furnace, I don't even realize how much I've put away until it's all gone and I'm sitting there feeling like I'm going to burst.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these episodes because you’re embarrassed about how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was touching on earlier, that is exactly why I do it alone. It's the embarrassment, plain and simple. I mean, picture this: we're sitting at the table, right? She's made a beautiful pot roast, maybe some of those glazed carrots she does, the house smells fantastic, and I'm sitting there acting like I'm just not that hungry. I'll literally put on a performance. I'll say, \"Oh honey, this is great, but they had a big spread down at the cafeteria today for somebody's retirement,\" or whatever lie comes to mind. I'll take a tiny little portion, practically eating like a bird, pushing it around my plate. \n\nBut on the inside? I'm starving. I just know that if I ate what I really wanted to eat right then and there, she would be horrified. I mean, she'd probably think I had a tapeworm or something, the way I can put it away. So I just can't do it in front of her. I'd be way too embarrassed for her—or anyone, really—to see how much I'm actually putting away. \n\nSo I just wait it out. I sit there and I wait until she's distracted, maybe she's watching her crime shows in the living room or she's on the phone with her sister, and then I'll make my excuse and sneak out to the truck or the workshop. I have to be alone. If I'm alone, there's no judgment, no wide eyes staring at me wondering when I'm going to stop. It's just me hidden away, because the thought of an audience for that... man, I couldn't handle the shame of it. I really couldn't. It's just too much to explain, so it's easier to just hide it out in the driveway.\n\n[Last Question]\nAfter these eating episodes, do you often feel disgusted with yourself, depressed, or guilty about what you've eaten?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, God... yeah. Disgusted doesn't even... I mean, that's exactly it. The second I take that last bite, or when there's just nothing left in the bag, that frantic feeling I was telling you about? It just vanishes entirely, and it’s replaced by this... this heavy, sickening wave of shame. I just sit there in the cab of the truck, or on my stool out by the workbench, looking at the crumpled up wrappers and the empty cups, and I feel so incredibly ashamed and kind of disgusted with myself. 

Because, you see, that's not who I am. Or, well, it's not who I ever thought I was. I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined guy, you know? A dependable man. I'm fifty-four years old. I've worked hard my whole life. I show up for my shifts at the plant day in and day out, I put in the overtime when they ask, I pay the mortgage, I take care of the yard... I'm the guy people are supposed to be able to count on. If something breaks, I fix it. If there's a problem, I handle it. I've always prided myself on having a strong work ethic and being a solid, reliable husband. 

But then... then I look at what I've just done. I'm sitting out there in the dark, wiping grease off my mouth with a paper napkin, terrified my wife is going to turn on the patio light. It just makes me feel like I’m sneaking around like some kind of criminal or a teenager breaking curfew. It's pathetic, is what it is. I'm completely letting myself down. I just sit there and think, how can you be the guy who handles all that responsibility at work, who prides himself on being in control, but you can't even stop yourself from hiding out in the garage stuffing your face? 

And that guilt, doc, it doesn't just go away when I finally clean up the mess and go inside. It hangs over me all night. I'll walk back in the house, and my wife will smile at me from the couch, completely oblivious, and I just feel like this giant fraud. I'll go into the bathroom to wash my hands and I can't even look myself in the eye in the mirror. I just stand there thinking about how I'm lying to her, and lying to myself, and how I'm just throwing away all my discipline for a few minutes of... whatever that is out there. It really eats at you. It just makes you feel so small, you know? Like you're not the man you thought you were at all, and it's a really dark place to be sitting in, mentally, just turning that over and over in your head before you have to wake up and do it all again the next day.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you for seeing me. I... I guess I'm here because my eating has just gotten way out of hand lately. You see, I work down at the production plant, and they've had me on these late shifts for a while now. It really messes with your body clock, you know? You get off work, it's pitch black out, you're completely exhausted, your feet are killing you... and well, I'll stop for takeout on the way home. \n\nI always tell myself it'll just be a quick bite to take the edge off, but then I get to the drive-thru and I end up ordering, like, two whole burgers, a huge order of fries, a thick shake, and I'll just put it all away in the car. And you'd think I'd be completely stuffed after all that, right? But then I get home, and I don't even go straight inside the house to go to bed. I go into the garage—we have this second refrigerator out there where we keep the extra groceries and Tupperware stuff—and I'll just stand there and clean out whatever leftovers I can find. It's literally like I'm eating a whole second supper right there in the garage. I just... I don't know why I do it, but it's becoming a real problem and I just can't seem to stop myself once I get going.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of overeating, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to 'undo' the calories you consumed?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I know some people do, you see it on TV or read about it in magazines, where folks will make themselves sick or take a bunch of pills to clear themselves out. But I've never done that. I couldn't stomach the thought of making myself throw up, to be honest. It's just not something I could bring myself to do. \n\nAnd as for exercise... well, by the time I've finished my shift at the plant, and then had my little drive-thru feast and my garage raid, I'm just too wiped out. My knees aren't what they used to be, you know? Being on concrete floors for eight, sometimes ten hours a day, it really takes a toll on the joints. So the idea of going for a run or hitting a gym after all that eating... it's just out of the question. I usually just end up dragging myself into the house, feeling so full and heavy, and just collapsing into my recliner. I'll sit there, feeling miserable about what I just did, my stomach stretched tight like a drum, but I just sit there and let it settle. I don't try to get rid of it or work it off, I just... sit with it. And feel terrible about it.\n\nDoctor: During these times when you're eating a lot, do you feel like you're losing control — like you can't stop eating even if you want to?\n\nPatient: That is exactly it. It's... well, it's honestly the most frustrating part of the whole ordeal, because I know what I'm doing while I'm doing it, if that makes any sense. Like, I'm fully aware that I've already eaten enough to feed a family of four in the front seat of my truck, but once I start, it feels like a switch is just flipped in my brain. \n\nI'll be standing out there in the chilly garage, staring into the fridge, and my mind is practically screaming at me to stop. I'm literally standing there, telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just close the refrigerator door, turn off the light, and go to bed.' I mean, I'm a grown man, you know? I'm fifty-four years old, I manage a whole section of guys down at the plant, I make decisions all night long, so I should be able to just walk away from a piece of Tupperware, right? \n\nBut it's like... it's like my hands don't even belong to me anymore. They just keep reaching for more. I'll grab another piece of cold chicken, or scoop out some leftover casserole, and I'm chewing and swallowing while that voice in my head is just begging me to quit. But I can't. The brakes are completely gone. I just keep going and going until there's nothing left in the container, even though I'm so full it physically hurts and I'm exhausted from my shift. It's a terrible feeling, just watching yourself do something you desperately want to stop doing, but you just... you just can't seem to make your hands stop moving.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control while eating happen? Are they something that happens multiple times a week, or more spread out?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about how often, because I was actually just trying to figure that out myself the other day. I was looking at my bank statements—because, let me tell you, all those drive-thru runs really start to add up, and my wife, she handles most of the finances, so I have to be careful about what I'm putting on the card—and I realized it's been happening pretty consistently for the past few months now. \n\nI'd say, at the very least, there's always one night a week where I just completely lose it. Usually, it's a Friday or whatever my Friday happens to be on that week's rotation, when the exhaustion just really catches up to me. But honestly, more often than not lately, it's been creeping up to two, sometimes even three times a week. It really depends on how the shifts are going. Like, if we're doing a big push to meet a quota and the line is just moving non-stop, and my supervisor is breathing down my neck... those are the nights I'm almost guaranteed to pull into that drive-thru and then hit the garage fridge. \n\nIt's like, the rougher the day, the more likely I am to end up standing out there in the cold, eating cold casserole out of a plastic tub. And it's just been a steady pattern for months now. I keep hoping it'll slow down when the schedule lightens up, but then we get a new order in, or someone calls out sick and I have to cover, and before I know it, I'm right back in that same cycle, two or three nights a week, just eating until I can't breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating make you feel very upset, interfere with your daily life, or affect things like your relationships or how you function at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot of things, really. Upsetting? Yeah, I'd say that's an understatement. Like I said, I sit there in my recliner and just feel terrible. It eats at me, no pun intended. But as for my daily life, it's complicated. I mean, I haven't missed a shift at the plant. I'm still clocking in, doing my job, making sure the line keeps moving. I take my responsibilities seriously, you know? And I still go to church on Sundays with my wife, shake hands, smile, do all the things you're supposed to do. I haven't let it completely derail my life in that sense. \n\nBut at home... that's where things are getting, well, strained. I've been pulling away, being a lot more withdrawn. And it's exhausting, honestly. It takes so much energy just to cover my tracks. I'll get home after my drive-thru run and my garage binge, and my wife will have dinner waiting, or she'll ask if I want to eat, and I have to come up with these excuses. I'll tell her I had a big lunch, or that my stomach is feeling a little off from something I ate at the cafeteria, or that I'm just too tired to eat right now. And it's lying. I'm lying to my wife, who I love dearly, just to hide what I'm doing. \n\nAnd then I have to make sure the evidence is gone. I'm burying fast-food wrappers at the bottom of the trash can outside, rearranging the Tupperware in the fridge so it doesn't look like half the casserole is missing... it's like a full-time job just keeping the secret. So, yeah, I'm functioning, I'm working, I'm showing up to church, but at home, I'm just... I'm not really there. I'm too busy managing this whole hidden life, and it's definitely putting a wall up between me and my wife. It's just a heavy, heavy thing to carry around all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes ever caused you to avoid social situations, feel embarrassed about your eating habits, or affected your performance or concentration at work?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as work goes, no, I wouldn't say it's affecting my performance or my concentration on the floor. Like I said, I take my job down at the plant very seriously. When I'm on the clock, I'm focused on the line, making sure my guys are doing what they need to do, checking the machinery... I don't really let my mind wander to food when I'm in the middle of a shift. The problem is always after the shift, you know? \n\nBut as for feeling embarrassed and avoiding social things... yeah. Yeah, absolutely. That's a huge part of it. And it's not even just big social events, it's right in my own house. I mean, my wife is a wonderful cook, she really is, and she always makes sure there's a good, hot meal ready. But I'll sit there at the table with her, and I'll just kind of pick at my food, or I'll take a really small portion. I'll put on this whole act, saying things like, \"Oh, I'm just not that hungry tonight,\" or \"I had a really heavy lunch at the cafeteria,\" when the truth is, I'm starving. But I can't eat the way I want to eat in front of her. I just can't. \n\nI know if I started eating the amount I actually want to eat, she'd look at me like I'd lost my mind. I'd be so incredibly embarrassed for her to see how much I'm actually putting away. So, I play it cool at the dinner table, act like a bird picking at seeds, and then... then I wait. I'll wait until she's busy watching her shows in the living room, or until she goes upstairs to take a bath, or sometimes I'll even just make an excuse to go out to the truck to 'check on something.' And that's when I do it. That's when I eat alone, where nobody can see me, because the thought of anyone, especially my wife, watching me shovel all that food into my mouth... it's just humiliating. It really is. I just can't bear the thought of anyone seeing me like that, completely out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes happen even when you’re not particularly stressed or tired, or do they usually happen only after a really rough day at work or other stressful situations?\n\nPatient: You know, that's a really good question, and honestly... I don't rightly know. I mean, I sit here and try to turn that over in my head, and it's hard to say because, well, what even is a day without stress or being tired lately? Working down at the plant, even on a supposedly \"good\" day, you're still standing on your feet for eight, sometimes ten hours straight on that hard concrete floor. Your lower back aches, your knees are barking by the time you finally clock out. So there's always some baseline level of exhaustion, you know? It's just part of the job, part of getting older, I guess. \n\nBut... do I do it when I'm actually feeling completely fine? When I'm rested and relaxed? I'm trying to think back to, like, a regular Saturday. Saturdays, if I don't pick up an overtime shift, we usually just take it easy. Me and the wife might putter around the house, I might mow the lawn or finally get around to fixing that squeaky hinge on the back screen door... just normal, quiet, everyday stuff. Am I stressed then? Not really. Not at all, actually. But do I still end up sneaking out to the garage fridge later that night? I... I'm just not sure. I haven't really paid close enough attention to whether it only happens after the rough shifts. \n\nLike I said before, those bad days with the quotas and the supervisor breathing down my neck, those are definitely the times I know for a fact I'm going to lose it. But as for the good days, or the easy days... I just haven't noticed if there's a strict rule to it. It all kind of blurs together after a while, to be perfectly honest with you. You get into this routine, this cycle of hiding and eating, and it just becomes a habit, almost like muscle memory. So, I guess I really don't have a solid answer for you on that one. I'd have to really stop and think about it and maybe track if it happens on a quiet weekend.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much faster than usual, or continuing to eat even when you're uncomfortably full?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Faster than usual doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... it's almost like a panic, you know? When I pull into the driveway after a shift, if I've stopped at the drive-thru, I'll usually just stay parked right there in the cab of my truck. Or, if I'm already home, I'll take whatever I scrounged up out to my workshop in the backyard, tell my wife I need to organize my wrenches or check on a project. And once I'm out there... man, I just tear into it. \n\nI eat so fast in my truck or out in the workshop that half the time I barely taste it. It's just... shove it in, chew once or twice, swallow, grab the next thing. It's exactly like I’m trying to get it down before somebody catches me. Because that's the real fear, right? I'm sitting out there, constantly looking over my shoulder, watching the back windows of the house to see if the kitchen blinds move or if the porch light clicks on. If I hear the neighbor's dog bark or the wind rattle the aluminum siding on the garage, my heart just jumps right up into my throat. \n\nSo I'm just rushing, rushing, rushing. I'll have a sandwich in one hand and a handful of whatever else in the other, and I'm not even enjoying the meal. You'd think if a guy is going to go through all this trouble, all this lying and sneaking around to get the food, he'd at least sit back and savor it, wouldn't you? But no. It's just this frantic blur. I'll end up getting grease on my steering wheel or dropping crumbs all over my workbench because I'm just not paying attention to anything except making the food disappear as fast as humanly possible. I'm just terrified she's going to walk out that back screen door and catch me stuffing my face like a madman. And because I'm eating at that kind of speed, just shoveling it in like coal into a furnace, I don't even realize how much I've put away until it's all gone and I'm sitting there feeling like I'm going to burst.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these episodes because you’re embarrassed about how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was touching on earlier, that is exactly why I do it alone. It's the embarrassment, plain and simple. I mean, picture this: we're sitting at the table, right? She's made a beautiful pot roast, maybe some of those glazed carrots she does, the house smells fantastic, and I'm sitting there acting like I'm just not that hungry. I'll literally put on a performance. I'll say, \"Oh honey, this is great, but they had a big spread down at the cafeteria today for somebody's retirement,\" or whatever lie comes to mind. I'll take a tiny little portion, practically eating like a bird, pushing it around my plate. \n\nBut on the inside? I'm starving. I just know that if I ate what I really wanted to eat right then and there, she would be horrified. I mean, she'd probably think I had a tapeworm or something, the way I can put it away. So I just can't do it in front of her. I'd be way too embarrassed for her—or anyone, really—to see how much I'm actually putting away. \n\nSo I just wait it out. I sit there and I wait until she's distracted, maybe she's watching her crime shows in the living room or she's on the phone with her sister, and then I'll make my excuse and sneak out to the truck or the workshop. I have to be alone. If I'm alone, there's no judgment, no wide eyes staring at me wondering when I'm going to stop. It's just me hidden away, because the thought of an audience for that... man, I couldn't handle the shame of it. I really couldn't. It's just too much to explain, so it's easier to just hide it out in the driveway.\n\nDoctor: After these eating episodes, do you often feel disgusted with yourself, depressed, or guilty about what you've eaten?\n\nPatient: Oh, God... yeah. Disgusted doesn't even... I mean, that's exactly it. The second I take that last bite, or when there's just nothing left in the bag, that frantic feeling I was telling you about? It just vanishes entirely, and it’s replaced by this... this heavy, sickening wave of shame. I just sit there in the cab of the truck, or on my stool out by the workbench, looking at the crumpled up wrappers and the empty cups, and I feel so incredibly ashamed and kind of disgusted with myself. \n\nBecause, you see, that's not who I am. Or, well, it's not who I ever thought I was. I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined guy, you know? A dependable man. I'm fifty-four years old. I've worked hard my whole life. I show up for my shifts at the plant day in and day out, I put in the overtime when they ask, I pay the mortgage, I take care of the yard... I'm the guy people are supposed to be able to count on. If something breaks, I fix it. If there's a problem, I handle it. I've always prided myself on having a strong work ethic and being a solid, reliable husband. \n\nBut then... then I look at what I've just done. I'm sitting out there in the dark, wiping grease off my mouth with a paper napkin, terrified my wife is going to turn on the patio light. It just makes me feel like I’m sneaking around like some kind of criminal or a teenager breaking curfew. It's pathetic, is what it is. I'm completely letting myself down. I just sit there and think, how can you be the guy who handles all that responsibility at work, who prides himself on being in control, but you can't even stop yourself from hiding out in the garage stuffing your face? \n\nAnd that guilt, doc, it doesn't just go away when I finally clean up the mess and go inside. It hangs over me all night. I'll walk back in the house, and my wife will smile at me from the couch, completely oblivious, and I just feel like this giant fraud. I'll go into the bathroom to wash my hands and I can't even look myself in the eye in the mirror. I just stand there thinking about how I'm lying to her, and lying to myself, and how I'm just throwing away all my discipline for a few minutes of... whatever that is out there. It really eats at you. It just makes you feel so small, you know? Like you're not the man you thought you were at all, and it's a really dark place to be sitting in, mentally, just turning that over and over in your head before you have to wake up and do it all again the next day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "diagnosis": "F50.8",
